Fluent APIs in Functional Languages (full version) Journal: PACMPL Volume: 1 CONF 1 1 CCS: Software and its engineering API languages CCS: Software and its engineering Functional languages
Ori Roth, Yossi Gil

Abstract
. Fluent API is an object-oriented pattern for elegant APIs and embedded DSLs. A smart fluent API can enforce the API protocol or DSL syntax at compile time. As fluent API designs typically rely on function overloading, they are hard to realize in functional programming languages. We show how to write functional fluent APIs using parametric polymorphism and type inference instead of overloading. Our designs support all regular and deterministic context-free API protocols and beyond.



1

1.
Introduction

An API protocol dictates a set of rules for correct API usage.
Misusing an API to contrast its protocol may result in runtime errors or undefined behavior.
The Java
FileReader
API protocol, for example, states that

“once the stream has been closed, further [API] invocations will throw an
IOException
.’’
1
1

1

https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/io/InputStreamReader.html#close--

Although protocols usually come in written form as part of the API documentation, some protocols can be formalized as grammars.
Such grammar defines the language (set) of legal sequences of API invocations.
For example, the following regular expression describes the
FileReader
protocol:

{{{{{\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{}{$\langle$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{initialize}}}{\@listingGroup{}{$\rangle$}}}}}}}\;\;\;\big(\;\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{read}}}}}}}}~\big|~\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{skip}}}}}}}}~\big|~\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{reset}}}}}}}}\;\big)^{*}\;\;\;\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{close}}}}}}}}^{*}

Specifying API protocols using grammars or automata reduces the problem of protocol verification to formal language recognition.

APIs also use grammars and automata to specify domain-specific languages (DSLs).
A DSL is a specialized language tailored for the API’s problem domain.
Many applications use DSLs:
A Python machine learning model uses SQL to load its dataset, a JavaScript frontend verifies textual forms with regular expressions, and a Standard ML (SML) HTTP server serves HTML webpages.
To integrate a DSL into an application, we can implement the DSL as an API in the host programming language.
The protocol of this embedded DSL (EDSL) is the syntax of the original DSL, i.e., EDSL invocations must describe well-formed DSL programs.

This paper presents methods to create
smart
and
elegant
APIs in functional programming languages.
These APIs enforce their protocols or DSL syntax at compile time by performing language recognition at the type level.
For example,
demonstrates how we embed an HTML document as an SML expression using our methodology.

Listing 1:
An HTML webpage embedded in SML

Our HTML EDSL is
elegant
in the sense that embedded webpages closely resemble HTML, with minimal syntactic overhead imposed by the host programming language.
The EDSL is
smart
as it coerces the SML compiler into enforcing HTML’s syntax, so invalid webpages do not compile.
The tags must appear in the correct order (
<
html
>
before
<
body
>
,
<
tr
>
inside
<
table
>
, and so on), every opening tag must have a matching closing tag, and all table rows must have the same number of columns.
Note that the additional requirement on table rows elevates HTML into a context-sensitive language.

Our API designs are based on
fluent API
, an object-oriented pattern for embedding DSLs.
Fluent API techniques typically rely on function overloading, a common object-oriented feature not supported by many functional type systems.
We adapt fluent APIs to functional settings by replacing overloading with parametric polymorphism and type inference.
Our fluent APIs do not rely on a specific language feature, so they are compatible with most statically-typed functional languages.

1.1.
Related Work

A typestate
(
Strom:1986
)
determines the type’s set of permitted operations in a given program context.
The
FileReader
typestate, for example, disallows invoking
read
after
close
is called.
Typestates are used as a first-class abstraction for protocols
(
Aldrich:2009
;
Garcia:2014
;
Sunshine:2011
;
DeLine:2001
;
Degen:2007
;
Kuncak:2002
)
.
Another approach is to infer and verify typestates and API protocols statically
(
Fink:2008
;
Bodden:2012
;
Field:2003
;
Pradel:2012
)
.

Ferles:2021
developed a static analysis method for enforcing API protocols specified by context-free grammars.
Similarly, the current work uses automata specifications for API protocols.
Instead of using an external tool or a dedicated language feature, our fluent API designs use the host language compiler to enforce the protocol by ensuring that protocol violations result in type errors.

Language workbenches
(
Fowler:language:workbenches
;
Erdweg:2013
)
such as Xtext
(
Eysholdt:2010
)
, MPS
(
Voelter:2012
)
, and MetaEdit+
(
Kelly:1996
)
are development environments for creating DSL development tools.
The Spoofax language workbench, for instance, compiles DSL specifications into a full-fledged DSL IDE
(
Kats:2010
)
.
SugarJ
(
Erdweg:2011
)
, Polyglot
(
Nystrom:2003
)
, and Racket
(
Felleisen:2015
)
are examples of extensible programming languages in which the syntax of the language can be extended to incorporate DSLs.
By embedding the DSL as a fluent API, our techniques work out of the box in statically-typed functional languages.

Programming languages with powerful compile-time metaprogramming support provide a direct solution to DSL embedding
(
Czarnecki:2004
)
.
MetaML
(
Sheard:1999
)
, MetaOCaml
(
Calcagno:2003
;
Kiselyov:2014
)
, and Template Haskell
(
Sheard:2002
)
extend existing programming languages with macro systems that enable DSL code generation at compile time.
Evidently, Haskell is expressive enough to embed DSLs even without templates.
Haskell supports ad hoc polymorphism through type classes
(
Wadler:1989
)
, shown to be useful for DSL embedding
(
Augustsson:2008
;
Hudak:1998
)
.
EDSLs often employ Haskell’s
do
-statement and monadic comprehensions
(
Gill:2014
;
Bracker:2014
)
, especially in parser combinator libraries
(
Leijen:2000
;
Hutton:1998
;
Leijen:2001
;
Florijn:1995
)
.
In contrast to previous approaches, functional fluent APIs do not rely on any macro system, language extension, or special control structure.
Some of the Haskell EDSL techniques are ad hoc, whereas our fluent API solutions support well-defined classes of DSLs, namely all regular and deterministic context-free languages.

Existing fluent API designs generally do not apply to functional languages because of their lack of function overloading.
Enhancing functional languages with ad hoc polymorphism, e.g., as proposed by
Bourdoncle:1997
,
Shields:2001
, and
Neubauer:2002
, may enable these designs to work in functional settings.

Yamazaki:2019
managed to encode fluent APIs in Haskell by using typeclasses and a few language extensions.
Their work and other modern fluent API designs are discussed in
Section

2
.
Our fluent APIs overcome the overloading problem by avoiding it altogether in favor of rudimentary functional features.

Indeed, we chose SML as the main language used throughout the paper for its relative simplicity.
Noting SML’s rich module system
(
MacQueen:1984
)
, we limit ourselves to core language constructs
(
Harper:1986
)
.
Fluent API in SML is not a new concept:

Kamin:1997
presented a fluent API-like EDSL eight years before the term was even coined
(
Fowler:2005
)
.

1.2.
Contributions

We present different algorithms for encoding fluent APIs in functional languages.
Our fluent API designs support all regular and deterministic context-free API protocols and DSLs and can be implemented in most statically-typed functional languages.
This work is accompanied by an SML implementation of key algorithms.
The appendix includes a series of experiments conducted using the implementation.

Outline

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.

Section

2
covers preliminary knowledge in automata theory and object-oriented fluent APIs.
The next three sections present three methods for encoding regular protocols as functional fluent APIs:

bit shuffling
in
,
Church Booleans
in
, and
tabulation
in
.
In
we show how to extend these techniques to non-regular protocols.

concludes with a discussion on functional fluent APIs and a comparison of the various encoding methods.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Automata and Formal Languages

We assume that the reader is familiar with the structure and operation of deterministic finite state machines (FSMs) and pushdown automata (DPDA).
Here we briefly review some definitions;
for the full theoretical background, we recommend
Hopcroft:2007
.

FSMs recognize the class of regular languages.
An FSM
M
is a tuple
M=\langle Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\delta,F\rangle
, where
Q
is a finite set of states,
\Sigma
is a finite set of input letters,
q_{0}\in Q
is the initial state,
\delta:Q\times\Sigma\rightarrow Q
is the (partial) transition function, and
F\subseteq Q
is the set of accepting states.
Let
\delta_{\sigma}:Q\rightarrow Q
be
\delta
’s projection on
\sigma
, i.e.,
\delta(q,\sigma)=p\Leftrightarrow\delta_{\sigma}(q)=p
.

The run of word
w=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\ldots\sigma_{n}\in\Sigma^{*}
on FSM
M
is a sequence of states
\rho=p_{0}p_{1}\cdots p_{n}
such that
p_{0}=q_{0}
and for every
i=1,\ldots,n
,
\delta(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i})=p_{i}
.
We say that
M
accepts
w
if and only if
p_{n}\in F
; otherwise
M
rejects
w
.
The language of
M
,
L(M)
is the set of words
M
accepts.
Consider, for example, the FSM depicted in
Fig.

2.1
in graph form.
This FSM recognizes the language
(a^{4})^{*}
containing all sequences of the letter
a
whose length is a multiple of four.

q_{0}
start
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
a
a
a
a

Figure 2.1
.
An FSM accepting the language
(a^{4})^{*}

A DPDA is an FSM that uses an auxiliary pushdown store
of stack symbols drawn from a finite set
\Gamma
.
The DPDA transition function is

\delta:Q\times(\Sigma\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times\Gamma\rightarrow Q\times\Gamma^{*}

In each computation step, the automaton reads symbol
\gamma\in\Gamma
on the stack and replaces it with a (possibly empty) sequence of symbols
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma^{*}
.
If the the automaton tries to read from an empty stack, it gets stuck and rejects the input word.
A DPDA transition can consume an input letter
\sigma\in\Sigma
or
\varepsilon
, the empty word.
These
\varepsilon
-transitions are used to modify the stack between input reads.
A DPDA without
\varepsilon
-transitions is called a real-time DPDA, as it makes exactly one operation for each input letter.
DPDAs recognize the class of deterministic context-free languages (DCFLs), a proper super-set of regular languages.
For instance, a DPDA can recognize the Dyck language of balanced brackets, but an FSM cannot.

2.2.
Encoding Automata as Fluent APIs in Object-Oriented Programming Languages

The traditional study of compiler design
(
Aho:1977
)
provides many parser generation techniques that take formal language specifications and convert them into parsers.
Similarly, the academic study of fluent APIs seeks DSL embedding techniques that encode DSL specifications (usually in automaton form) as fluent APIs.
We now review some of the existing fluent API encoding methods, designed primarily for object-oriented host languages.

Encoding an FSM as a fluent API is easy.
First, create class
q
for each state
q\in Q
.
Then, for each transition
\delta(q,\sigma)=p
, add the method
\sigma
:
q
\rightarrow
p
to class
q
.
This method receives an (implicit) parameter of type
q
and returns an instance of class
p
.
Finally, add a special method
$
only to classes of accepting states
q_{F}\in F
(its return type does not matter).
A fluent chain of API calls (chain for short) starts with an initial variable
\
_
\
_
of type
q_{0}
, followed by a sequence of API calls
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\in\Sigma
, and ends with a call to
$
:

(1)

__
().
\sigma_{1}
().
\sigma_{2}
().
\ldots
.
\sigma_{n}
().
$
()

For example, the fluent API in
encodes the language
(a^{4})^{*}
as defined by the FSM in
Fig.

2.1
.
The fluent API is followed by several chain examples in the form of
Eq.

1
.
A given chain type checks if and only if the number of times it calls
a
()
is a multiple of four.
We say that the fluent API enforces (encodes, specifies, recognizes) the language
(a^{4})^{*}
.

}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x1}}}\textgreater}}}}.Notethatthetreeencodingalgorithm,aswellastheencodingtechniquesproposedinthepaperscitedabove,relyonoverloadingsimilarlytotheFSMcase.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{A Note on Typeclasses and Fluent APIs}
\par The object-oriented fluent API techniques mentioned above, including the more advanced designs of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Gil:2019}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Yamazaki:2019}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, can be adapted to functional programming languages by substituting typeclasses for function overloading.
Typeclasses were introduced by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Wadler:1989}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} as a formalism for ad hoc polymorphism in Haskell and other functional programming languages.
Wadler and Blott defined the semantics of typeclasses by translating them to the standard Hindley-Milner type system.
This translation method was later called \emph{dictionary passing}.
\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{listing:dictionary:passing} demonstrates how to use dictionary passing to implement an ``overloaded'' function in SML.
\par\par{\@listings{\@@listings@block{4}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize\@lst@startline{\lx@tags{\lx@tag@intags[]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[autoref]{1\hbox{}}\lx@tag@intags[creftype]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypecap]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypeplural]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypepluralcap]{}\lx@tag@intags[refnum]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[typerefnum]{{line SML}}}}}}\par Function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{square}}}}}}} in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{listing:dictionary:passing} accepts integers and real numbers.
We tell \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{square}}}}}}} how to multiply numbers by passing it a dictionary \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{numD}}}}}}} that points to the appropriate multiplication method: \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{intD}}}}}}} for integers and \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{realD}}}}}}} for real numbers.
\par Although dictionary passing is semantically equivalent to typeclasses, it is not a viable alternative for function overloading.
Matching each argument type with a dictionary (\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{intD}}}}}}}, \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{realD}}}}}}}) takes the same effort from the programmer as having multiple functions (\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{squareInt}}}}}}}, \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{squareReal}}}}}}}).
In Wadler and Blott\textquoteright s original design, the compiler infers the dictionary from the argument type, so the programmer doesn't have to specify it explicitly.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Bit Shuffling}
The fluent API encoding techniques described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{section:fluent} use function overloading and, therefore, cannot be used in functional languages such as SML.
This section presents \emph{bit shuffling}, the first of our three alternative methods for encoding FSMs as functional fluent APIs.
This method is unique in that it does not rely on unification, resulting in relatively fast compilation even when enforcing highly complex API protocols.
\par The crux of the new design is seeing the FSM states as binary vectors and the FSM transitions as a collection of Boolean functions over these vectors.
The current FSM state is encoded using a tuple of types \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}}}}} and \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}}}}} that represent the bits 0 and 1.
Each API function applies a certain transformation onto this tuple by \emph{shuffling} the bits comprising it:
$$\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}})}}}}\implies\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}})}}}}$$If the shuffling is done correctly, the resulting tuple encodes the subsequent FSM state.
\par We start by constructing a fluent API for the language $(a^{4})^{*}$, as defined by the FSM in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{figure:fsm:a4} (this will be our running example).
Then, we generalize the construction steps for any FSM and assemble them into \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{algorithm:shuffle}.
\par First, however, we define some notations.
A Boolean bit $b$ is a member of $\{0,1\}$.
We also denote Boolean bits as \emph{false}, \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}}}}}, and \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{F}}}}}}} or \emph{true}, \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}}}}}, and \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{T}}}}}}}.
A Boolean vector $v$ of length $n$ is a sequence of $n$ Boolean bits, $v\in\{0,1\}^{n}$.
We write $v$ as $\langle b_{n},\ldots,b_{2},b_{1}\rangle$ (indexed from the right to the left) or as $b_{n}\cdots b_{2}b_{1}$ for short.
An $n$-ary Boolean function $f:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}$ accepts a Boolean vector of length $n$ and returns one Boolean bit.
An $n$-ary Boolean vector function $\boldsymbol{f}:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}$ accepts a Boolean vector of length $n$ and returns a vector of the same length.
A Boolean vector function can be decomposed into $n$ Boolean functions $\boldsymbol{f}=\langle f_{n},\ldots,f_{2},f_{1}\rangle$ such that $f_{i}$ computes the $i$\textsuperscript{{th}} bit of $\boldsymbol{f}$, $$\boldsymbol{f}(v)=\langle f_{n}(v),\ldots,f_{2}(v),f_{1}(v)\rangle$$The composition of Boolean function $g$ and vector function $\boldsymbol{f}$ is defined by
\begin{equation*}(g\circ\boldsymbol{f})(v)=g(\boldsymbol{f}(v))\end{equation*}Finally, the composition of Boolean vector functions $\boldsymbol{g}$ and $\boldsymbol{f}$ is defined as $$\boldsymbol{g}\circ\boldsymbol{f}=\langle g_{n}\circ\boldsymbol{f},\ldots,g_{2}\circ\boldsymbol{f},g_{1}\circ\boldsymbol{f}\rangle$$Note that $(\boldsymbol{g}\circ\boldsymbol{f})(v)=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{f}(v))$.
\par We are now ready to describe the FSM encoding steps.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Step I: Binary Encoding of States and Transitions}
\par Encode the automaton states $Q=\{q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}$ as Boolean vectors of length $\log(|Q|)=2$ using an arbitrary injective mapping $\beta:Q\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{2}$:
\begin{equation*}\begin{aligned} \beta(q_{0})&=00\\
\beta(q_{1})&=01\\
\beta(q_{2})&=10\\
\beta(q_{3})&=11\end{aligned}\end{equation*}Recall that $\delta_{a}$ is the projection of the transition function $\delta$ on letter $\sigma=a$.
Now, construct a Boolean vector function $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}$ such that $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(\beta(q))=\beta(p)\Leftrightarrow\delta_{a}(q)=p$:
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{f}^{a}(00)&=01\\
\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(01)&=10\\
\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(10)&=11\\
\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(11)&=00\end{aligned}\end{equation}\par Let $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}=\langle f^{a}_{2},f^{a}_{1}\rangle$.
Notice that $f^{a}_{2}$ is exclusive or (XOR), $f^{a}_{2}=\oplus$ and $f^{a}_{1}$ is negation (NOT) applied to the LSB, $f^{a}_{1}=\neg_{1}$, so we can write $$\boldsymbol{f}^{a}=\langle\oplus,\neg_{1}\rangle$$Observe that we can describe the FSM run on word $w=a^{n}$ using a sequence of $n$ applications of $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}$:
\begin{equation*}(\boldsymbol{f}^{a}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a}\circ\ldots\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a})(\beta(q_{0}))=\beta(p_{n})\end{equation*}where $p_{n}$ is the last state in $w$'s run
(this observation is proved by a simple induction on $n$).
We can say that $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}$ consolidates the four FSM transitions comprising $\delta_{a}$ into a single Boolean vector function.
\par Intuitively, the consolidation of FSM transitions into a single function helps us overcome the overloading problem, which arose in the first place because of the multiplicity of transitions.
It is not enough, however, to implement $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}$ as a function on Boolean vectors.
Let
\begin{equation}\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$\ldots$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\@listingLiterate{\textdollar}}}}}}\end{equation}be a functional fluent chain, with \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}}}}} replacing \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}}}}} as the initial variable (now a function).
We want the chain \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:shuffling:chain} to compile if and only if the number of times \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}}}}} is called is a multiple of four, i.e., if the chain encodes a word $w\in L((a^{4})^{*})$.
Yet, if we implement the fluent API as a product of Boolean vector functions, say,
\par{\@listings{\@@listings@block{5}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize\@lst@startline{\lx@tags{\lx@tag@intags[]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[autoref]{1\hbox{}}\lx@tag@intags[creftype]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypecap]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypeplural]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypepluralcap]{}\lx@tag@intags[refnum]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[typerefnum]{{line }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b2}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b1}}})}}}},nowhowever,dependsonthespecifictypesofitsarguments,whichbringsusbacktotheoverloadingproblem.WeaddressthisissueinStepII.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Step II: Direct Encoding of Boolean Function Evaluations}
\par Consider the following Boolean functions:
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} g^{1}(b_{2}b_{1})&=b_{2}\wedge b_{1}\\
g^{2}(b_{2}b_{1})&=b_{2}\wedge\neg b_{1}\\
g^{4}(b_{2}b_{1})&=\neg b_{2}\wedge b_{1}\\
g^{8}(b_{2}b_{1})&=\neg b_{2}\wedge\neg b_{1}\end{aligned}\end{equation}Function $g^{8}$ can be used to check our FSM's acceptance condition, since $$q\in F\iff q=q_{0}\iff g^{8}(\beta(q))=1$$In the bit shuffling method, the terminal function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textdollar}}}}} does not evaluate $g^{8}$ by itself, but instead receives its evaluation as an argument \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}}}}}} (to replace \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b2}}}}}}} and \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b1}}}}}}}).
Then, it is a simple matter to check that the type of \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}}}}}} is \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}}}}}, i.e., that $g^{8}(\beta(p_{n}))=1$.
\par Let $w=a^{n}$ be the word encoded by the fluent chain \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:shuffling:chain}, and let $\rho=p_{0}p_{1}\cdots p_{n}$ be its run.
The initial variable \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}}}}} assigns \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{T}}}}}}}, since $$g^{8}(\beta(p_{0}))=g^{8}(\beta(q_{0}))=1$$The API function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}}}}} should receive in parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}}}}}} the evaluation of $g^{8}(\beta(p_{i}))$ and return the evaluation of $g^{8}(\beta(p_{i+1}))$.
Since $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(\beta(q))=\beta(p)\Leftrightarrow\delta_{a}(q)=p$ (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:f}), $g^{8}(\beta(p_{i+1}))$ can be computed as follows: $$g^{8}(\beta(p_{i+1}))=g^{8}(\beta(\delta_{a}(p_{i})))=g^{8}(\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(\beta(p_{i})))=\ldots$$Let $\beta(p_{i})=b_{2}b_{1}$. We continue: $$\ldots=g^{8}(\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(b_{2}b_{1}))=g^{8}(\langle b_{2}\oplus b_{1},\neg b_{1}\rangle)=\neg(b_{2}\oplus b_{1})\wedge b_{1}=b_{2}\wedge b_{1}=g^{1}(b_{2}b_{1})=g^{1}(\beta(p_{i}))$$(Function $g^{1}$ is defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:gs}.)
Overall, we get \begin{equation}g^{8}(\beta(p_{i+1}))=(g^{8}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a})(\beta(p_{i}))=g^{1}(p_{i})\end{equation}i.e., the evaluation of $g^{8}$ on the next state $p_{i+1}$ is equal to applying the composition $g^{8}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a}$ to the current state $p_{i}$, which in turn equals the evaluation of another function $g^{1}$ on $p_{i}$.
\par We let \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}}}}} receive the value of $g^{1}(\beta(p_{i}))$ in yet another parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g1}}}}}}}.
We then continue recursively to compute $g^{1}(\beta(p_{i+1}))$, in a similar way to how $g^{8}(\beta(p_{i+1}))$ was computed:
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} g^{1}(\beta(p_{i+1}))=(g^{1}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a})(\beta(p_{i}))=g^{2}(\beta(p_{i}))\\
g^{2}(\beta(p_{i+1}))=(g^{2}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a})(\beta(p_{i}))=g^{4}(\beta(p_{i}))\\
g^{4}(\beta(p_{i+1}))=(g^{4}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a})(\beta(p_{i}))=g^{8}(\beta(p_{i}))\end{aligned}\end{equation}The recursion ends on $g^{8}(\beta(p_{i}))$, since this value is already given in parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}}}}}}.
Let $\boldsymbol{g}=\langle g^{1},g^{2},g^{4},g^{8}\rangle$ (the ordering of functions in $\boldsymbol{g}$ does not matter).
\lx@cref{creftypepluralcap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:g8} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:g1:g2:g4} can be summarized as follows:
\begin{equation}\boldsymbol{g}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{a}=\langle g^{2},g^{4},g^{8},g^{1}\rangle\end{equation}\par Thus, function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}}}}} receives a quadruple \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g1}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g2}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g4}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}})}}}} representing the four evaluations of $g^{j}(\beta(p_{i}))$, $j=1,2,4,8$, and returns the evaluations of $g^{j}(\beta(p_{i+1}))$ by shuffling the quadruple into \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g2}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g4}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g8}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g1}}})}}}} following \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:g}.
\par The resulting fluent API is shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{figure:fsm:a4}.
The addition of parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_string}{\color[rgb]{0.16,0,1}{\textquoteright\textquoteright}}}}}}} to functions \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}}}}} and \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}}}}} allows us to call them in fluent form, i.e., from the left to the right as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:shuffling:chain}.
\par\par{\@listings{\@@listings@block{7}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize\@lst@startline{\lx@tags{\lx@tag@intags[]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[autoref]{1\hbox{}}\lx@tag@intags[creftype]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypecap]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypeplural]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypepluralcap]{}\lx@tag@intags[refnum]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[typerefnum]{{line \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{figure:fsm:a4}}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{Listing\lx@nobreakspace 7}{A shuffling SML fluent API for the FSM of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde \in L((a^4)^*)$.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{The Bit Shuffling Encoding Algorithm}
\par We now generalize the encoding steps described in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{section:shuffling:1} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{section:shuffling:2} and present an algorithm for any FSM $M$.
FSMs are, in general, more complicated than our example FSM, so we need to add some minor steps to the encoding process.
First off, notice that our example FSM, depicted in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{figure:fsm:a4}, has a total transition function $\delta$, i.e., every state has an outgoing edge for each input letter, and the number of states $|Q|=4$ is a power of two.
These properties make the Boolean vector function $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}$ well-defined, i.e., $\boldsymbol{f}^{a}(v)$ is defined for any Boolean vector of length $n=\log(|Q|)$.
Given an FSM $M$, we make its transition function total by adding a non-accepting \emph{sink} state, to which the missing transitions are directed.
The sink state loops for any letter $\sigma\in\Sigma$.
We then complete the number of states to a power of two by, again, adding sink states.
These modifications result in automaton $M^{\prime}=\langle Q^{\prime},\Sigma,q_{0},F,\delta^{\prime}\rangle$ that accepts the same language as $M$, $L(M^{\prime})=L(M)$.
\par Let $n=\log(|Q^{\prime}|)$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$ be the number of bits in mapping $\beta$.
Our example fluent API required the evaluation of four Boolean functions, $g^{1}$, $g^{2}$, $g^{4}$, and $g^{8}$.
Instead of calculating which $g^{i}$ functions are required for $M^{\prime}$, we just take all the Boolean functions on $n$ bits, $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}=\langle g^{1},g^{2},g^{3},\ldots\rangle$ (there is a finite number of such functions).
In addition, notice that the example language $(a^{4})^{*}$ uses only a single letter $a$, but we want to support alphabets $\Sigma$ of any finite size.
Given alphabet $\Sigma$ of arbitrary size, we simply repeat the encoding steps described above for each letter $\sigma\in\Sigma$, i.e., compute $\boldsymbol{f}^{\sigma}$ and $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{\sigma}$ and encode the result in a function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{}{$\sigma$}}}}}}.
\par Finally, the example FSM specifies a single accepting state $F=\{q_{0}\}$, but FSMs in general may have multiple accepting states.
Therefore, we need to find a Boolean function $g^{F}$ such that $g^{F}(\beta(q))=1\Leftrightarrow q\in F$.
There must be (exactly one) such function in $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}$.
The terminal function simply checks that the type of parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gF}}}}}}}, holding the evaluation of $g^{F}$, is \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}}}}}.
\par The complete bit shuffling algorithm is presented in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{algorithm:shuffle}.
\par\begin{algorithm}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{1}}{The bit shuffling algorithm for encoding an FSM as an SML fluent API}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Algorithm 1}} {The bit shuffling algorithm for encoding an FSM as an SML fluent API}}}
\centering\fcolorbox{black!25}{white}{
\begin{minipage}{.91\textwidth}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\INPUT FSM $M=\langle Q, \Sigma, q_0, F, \delta\rangle$
\PRINT{\inline[style=sml]{datatype t = T and f = F}}
\STATE{Construct FSM $M'=\langle Q', \Sigma, q_0, F, \delta'\rangle$ from $M$ by making the transition function $\delta$ total and adding states until $|Q'|$ is a power of two (both using sink states)}
\LET{$n$}{$\log(|Q'|)$}
\LET{$\boldsymbol{g}^n$}{all Boolean functions on $n$ bits}
\LET{$\beta$}{an injective mapping $\beta:Q'\rightarrow\{0, 1\}^n$}
\PRINT{\inline[style=sml,literate=*{C}{\textasciicircum}{1}]{fun CC f' = f'\ $\boldsymbol{g}^n(\beta(q_0))$}}
\FORALL{$\sigma\in\Sigma$}
\LET{$\boldsymbol{f}^\sigma$}{a mapping $\{0, 1\}^n\rightarrow\{0, 1\}^n$ such that $\boldsymbol{f}^\sigma(\beta(q))=\beta(p) \Leftrightarrow\delta_\sigma(q)=p$}
\PRINT{\inline[style=sml]{fun\ $\sigma$\ $\boldsymbol{g}^n$ f' = f'\ $\boldsymbol{g}^n \circ\boldsymbol{f}^\sigma$}}
\ENDFOR\LET{$g^F$}{the (one and only) function in $\boldsymbol{g}^n$ such that $g^F(\beta(q))=1 \Leftrightarrow q \in F$}
\PRINT{\inline[style=sml,literate=*{D}{\textdollar}{1}{E}{\hbox to 1em{.\hss.\hss.}}{1}]{fun D (_, E, _, T, _, E, _) = ()} where \inline{T} appears in the position of $g^F$ in $\boldsymbol{g}^n$}
\end{algorithmic}
\end{minipage}}\@add@centering\end{algorithm}\par Some of the code excerpts printed in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{algorithm:shuffle} contain mathematical symbols---we now explain how these are rendered as text:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@A Boolean bit $b$ is rendered as \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{T}}}}}}} if $b=1$ or as \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{F}}}}}}} if $b=0$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@A Boolean vector $v=\langle b_{n},\ldots,b_{2},b_{1}\rangle$ is rendered as a tuple \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}({\@listingGroup{}{$b_{n}$}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\@listingLiterate{\hbox to8.00002pt{.\hss.\hss.}},\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$b_{2}$}},\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$b_{1}$}})}}}}.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@A Boolean function $g$ is rendered as \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{g}}}}}}}, i.e., we ignore its intent and print its name.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@A Boolean vector function $\boldsymbol{g}=\langle g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle$ is rendered as a tuple \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}({\@listingGroup{}{$g_{1}$}},\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$g_{2}$}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\@listingLiterate{\hbox to8.00002pt{.\hss.\hss.}},\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$g_{n}$}})}}}}.
\end{itemize}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Encoding Improvements}
\par We now propose two optional improvements of the bit shuffling algorithm as it is described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{algorithm:shuffle}.
First, notice that \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{algorithm:shuffle} computes all the binary functions on $n=\log(|Q^{\prime}|)$ bits.
Overall, there are $$2^{2^{n}}=2^{|Q^{\prime}|}\leq 2^{2|Q|}=4^{|Q|}$$such functions, so the run time of the algorithm, as well as the size of the code it prints, are exponential in the size of the FSM.
Nevertheless, our example fluent API (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{listing:shuffle:a4}) uses only four of these functions (out of 16), indicating that it might be possible to get rid of at least some functions.
To determine which functions are necessary and which can be omitted, we run the recursive procedure described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{section:shuffling:2}:
Start with $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}=\langle g^{F}\rangle$, and recursively add function $g$ if there exists $\sigma\in\Sigma$ and $g^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{g}^{n}$ such that $g^{\prime}\circ\boldsymbol{f}^{\sigma}=g$.
From our experience, this construction of $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}$ reduces its size significantly for ``reasonable'' FSMs.
Determining the exact degree to which $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}$ is reduced and, specifically, whether or not its sizes stays exponential in $|Q|$, is left as an open problem.
\par The second improvement relates to the fluent API user experience.
The fluent API printed by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{algorithm:shuffle} allows any sequence of API calls to be made and checks whether or not it makes sense only in the terminal method \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textdollar}}}}}.
Therefore, the programmer using the fluent API is not alerted when they call a wrong API method, and when they do (on calling \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textdollar}}}}}), they are not told where the mistake is located.
Let
\begin{equation}\text{\hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}\@listingLiterate{\textasciicircum}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$\sigma_{1}$}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$\sigma_{2}$}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$\ldots$}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{}{$\sigma_{n}$}}}}}}}\end{equation}be a partial fluent chain encoding $w=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{n}$, and let $\rho=p_{0}p_{1}\cdots p_{n}$ be $w$'s run on the FSM.
To make our fluent API more user friendly, we ensure that a call to $\sigma_{n+1}\in\Sigma$ after \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:fluent:partial} will not compile if the resulting chain cannot be completed correctly by some sequence of API calls.
We call this property of fluent APIs \emph{early failure}.
\par Let $R\subseteq Q$ be the set of states from which an accepting state is reachable (when viewing the FSM in graph form).
Then, the call to $\sigma_{n+1}$ should compile if and only if \begin{equation}\delta(p_{n},\sigma_{n+1})\in R\end{equation}We can check this condition using the Boolean function $g^{R}$ (similar to $g^{F}$ used by \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textdollar}}}}}) that holds $$g^{R}(\beta(q))=1\iff q\in R$$We require that the evaluation of $g^{R}$, stored in parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gR}}}}}}}, is always true throughout the chain to achieve early failure.
Let $\sigma\in\Sigma$ be an API function, and let \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gi}}}}}}} be the parameter it assigns in the position of \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gR}}}}}}} in the shuffled tuple on function $\sigma$'s right-hand side, i.e., $g^{R}(\beta(p_{j+1}))=g_{i}(\beta(p_{j}))$.
If $g^{R}$ evaluates to false after calling $\sigma$, then our encoding ensures that \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gi}}}}}}} is also false.
Function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{}{$\sigma$}}}}}} should, accordingly, check that the type of \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gi}}}}}}} is \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}}}}} by replacing the parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gi}}}}}}} with \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{T}}}}}}}, as done in function \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}\@listingLiterate{\textdollar}}}}} for parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gF}}}}}}}.
\par When the two improvements described above are applied together, the recursive construction of $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}$ should start with $\boldsymbol{g}^{n}=\langle g^{F},g^{R}\rangle$ to ensure that the parameter \hbox{\pagecolor{lighter-grey}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor{\@listingGroup{}{$\mathstrut$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{gR}}}}}}} is present in the final vector.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Church Booleans}
This section describes our fluent API encoding method based on the Church encoding of Booleans.
In contrast to bit shuffling, presented in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{section:shuffling}, the current technique relies on type inference and thus incurs longer compilation times.
Nevertheless, our technique demonstrates an application of the classic Church Booleans to API design which, we believe, is a fascinating concept whose full potential is beyond the scope of the current paper.
\par Recall that bit shuffling encodes FSM states as Boolean vectors and FSM transitions as functions over these vectors.
The same idea is at the base of our Church encoding.
But instead of pre-computing function evaluations, as done in bit shuffling, the new technique directly computes bit functions at compile time by replacing the true and false monotypes with the Church Booleans.
While Church Boolean circuits are usually applied in an untyped manner, they work mostly the same at the type level, except for a minor digression: duplicating a bit into two identical bits must be expressed explicitly using a fanout gate.
\par Before presenting the encoding, we review Church Booleans and how they can be implemented in SML:
\par{\@listings{\@@listings@block{8}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize\@lst@startline{\lx@tags{\lx@tag@intags[]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[autoref]{1\hbox{}}\lx@tag@intags[creftype]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypecap]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypeplural]{}\lx@tag@intags[creftypepluralcap]{}\lx@tag@intags[refnum]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[typerefnum]{{line
